And with the kids, I want to be there, I do, but I’m not sure I feel it the way I’m supposed to. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel pleasure or interest in activities that you're currently doing?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I still do things, you know? Like I’ll be out with the crew and there’s a yard where the lines come out straight, and for a second I notice it. But then my mind goes off to whether the edging is uneven or if we’re behind schedule.\n\nWith my kids, I want to care about what we’re doing, and I do care about them, obviously, but pleasure… interest… I don’t know. It’s like trying to tell if a plant’s alive in winter. Maybe there’s something there, but it’s hard for me to feel it the way I think I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most of the day, I’d say. It’s not always like crying sad, exactly. It’s more like I wake up and there’s already this gray heaviness sitting on me.\n\nThen I go out with the crew, and I’m mowing or trimming or checking a sprinkler head, and I’m doing the work, but it’s still there. Same when I’m with my kids… I’m there, but it feels kind of hollow, like I’m just moving through it instead of really being in it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but no longer find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can name it exactly. I mean, I used to do landscaping even when I wasn’t working sometimes—mess around in the yard, fix up a bed just because it looked bad—but now I mostly do it because it needs doing.\n\nBut then again, everybody gets tired of work after doing it for years, right? Like my knees hurt after a long day, and the mower we’ve got has been acting up, and one of the guys keeps leaving tools in the truck bed instead of putting them back where they go, so by the time I get home I don’t know if it’s that I don’t enjoy things or I’m just worn out.\n\nWith the kids, too… I don’t know. I’m there. I go. I do the thing. I just can’t tell if “pleasurable” is the word I’d use anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this gray heaviness and emptiness?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over three years, I guess. About three years and a few months.\n\nIt kind of started when the groundskeeping contract changed and our schedule got stretched out. Longer days, different pace, everything sort of took over. At first I thought I was just tired from work, because, you know, when you’re out there all day and the grass is growing faster than you can keep up with… but it never really lifted. So yeah, about three years and three months, if I’m counting right.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of even a few days where this gray heaviness lifted and you felt more like yourself?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I keep thinking maybe there were little breaks, like if we finished a big property early or the weather cooled off for a day, but the heaviness didn’t really go away.\n\nIt’s been there since that contract changed—about three years and three months ago, I guess. Once the longer days took over, it just kind of settled in. Even on days that should feel lighter, I don’t feel like I’m really back to myself.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've recently done that you felt gave you pleasure?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… recently? I mean, I’ve done things. I went over to my brother’s for a cookout, and I fixed this little patch by the side fence at home because the dirt was washing out, and it did look better after. But pleasure… that’s hard to say.\n\nSometimes you get a little relief when a job’s finished, like when the lines are clean and everything’s edged right, but then I’m already thinking about the next thing or what still needs doing. And my brother talks forever about his grill setup—he’s got all these opinions about charcoal versus propane, like it’s a religion—so I sort of just stand there and nod.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed something that felt pleasurable exactly. More like I’m just getting through it.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any recent situation where you felt genuinely happy or satisfied?\n\nPatient: Genuinely happy… I don’t know. I keep trying to think of something, like maybe when I’m with my kids or when the crew is joking around in the truck, you’d think that would do it.\n\nBut most days I wake up and it’s like this gray weight is already sitting on me before anything even happens. And then I go through the day, get out to the sites, talk to people, do what I’m supposed to do… but there’s this low, hollow feeling underneath it. Like I’m there, but I’m not really in it.\n\nI mean, sometimes I smile or laugh if somebody says something stupid at work, but satisfied? Happy? It doesn’t really stay. It’s more like I’m just moving through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall any activity, no matter how small, that you've done in the past few weeks where you felt a sense of enjoyment or satisfaction?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think… something small. I mean, there’s always little things I do. I sharpened the mower blades the other day, but that’s not really enjoyment, that’s just something that has to get done or else the cut looks bad and then people complain.\n\nI guess maybe when something looks neat for a second, like the edge along a walkway, I can look at it and think, “Alright, that’s clean.” But it doesn’t really feel like satisfaction the way I think you mean. It’s more like checking a box.\n\nAnd then my mind goes off to the next yard, or whether the truck needs work—because the truck’s been making this sound that I keep telling myself is probably nothing, but you know how that goes.\n\nSo… no, I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed enjoyment, not in a way that sticks out.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any lack of interest in activities you used to find enjoyable over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. Like… there are things I used to do, sure, but lately I don’t really think about whether I’m interested or not. I just kind of do what needs doing.\n\nI mean, I used to mess around more in the yard at home, not just work stuff, but my own little projects. Then again, when you do landscaping all day, the last thing you want is to come home and look at more edging and mulch. My neighbor’s always out there with his leaf blower like he’s clearing a runway, and I just think, man, how do you still have the energy for that?\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed a specific thing where I thought, “I don’t like this anymore.” It’s more like I don’t stop long enough to check.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days in the past two weeks where you felt interested in the things you were doing?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “interested” is a hard word. I show up and do the things, you know? If I’m laying sod or trimming hedges, I’m paying attention enough so I don’t mess it up or cut something I shouldn’t.\n\nBut interested like I’m into it? I can’t really say. Some days the guys start talking about equipment or whatever, and I’ll listen for a minute, but then I’m thinking about whether we’ve got enough gas cans or if somebody moved the rake again. There’s this one rake that keeps disappearing on the job sites, and I swear it’s like nobody knows how to put anything back.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really had a day where I noticed feeling interested. Mostly I’m just getting through what’s in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt a lack of pleasure in activities that normally would bring you joy over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: I guess… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. Like, “normally would bring me joy” — I’m not sure what even counts anymore. I do things because they’re there to do, or because if I don’t do them, they pile up.\n\nSometimes something should feel good, I suppose. Like finishing a yard and everything’s trimmed up right, or getting home and sitting down for a minute. But I don’t really stop and think, “Oh, this is pleasure.” Mostly I’m thinking about the next thing, or whether I forgot to load something back on the trailer. There was this one time we left a spool of line at a client’s place and had to drive all the way back, and ever since then I’m always checking the trailer twice.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed it in a clear way. I can’t really point to something and say, yeah, that used to bring me joy and now it doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days in the past two weeks where you felt completely back to normal?\n\nPatient: Completely back to normal? No… I don’t think so.\n\nI mean, I get up, I go to work, I’m around the crew, I see my kids, I do the regular stuff. From the outside it probably looks normal enough. But most days it’s like I wake up and there’s already this gray weight sitting on me. It’s hard to explain. Like I’m there, I’m doing things, but there’s this low, hollow feeling underneath it.\n\nEven when something’s happening around me — the guys joking on a job, or the kids talking about whatever they’re into — I’m kind of just going through the motions. Like I’m present but not really… all the way there. So no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had a day where I felt completely back to normal.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt a lack of pleasure in social interactions over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Social interactions… I don’t know. I mean, I talk to people because they’re there, or because we’re working and somebody has to say where the mower’s going or who’s grabbing the bags.\n\nPleasure, though… that’s kind of hard for me to pin down. Sometimes the guys will be joking around, and I’ll hear it, maybe even kind of smile, but then I’m thinking about whether we finished the back fence line or if the client wanted the clippings hauled off. People get real particular about that stuff, and then later they act like they told you when they didn’t.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I can’t say I’ve really noticed whether I’m getting pleasure out of talking to people. It’s mostly just something that happens while I’m getting through the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt a lack of pleasure in doing things that you normally enjoy over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that any different than before. I mean… I do things. I go through the list, I get stuff done. But “normally enjoy” is hard, because I don’t really keep a list of things I enjoy, you know?\n\nLike with work, sometimes a yard looks good when it’s done, the edges are clean, no weeds sticking up around the mailbox, and that should probably feel satisfying. But then I’m already thinking about the next place, or if the blower battery’s charged, or if one of the guys put the rake back in the wrong spot again. We had this whole mess once where a rake fell off because it wasn’t strapped right, and ever since then I’m always checking.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can’t really say I’ve noticed a clear lack of pleasure. I’m just kind of doing the things I do.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Any pleasure at all… I mean, maybe little bits, but I don’t really stop and think of it that way.

Like the other day one of the guys made some dumb joke about a sprinkler head looking like it was “saluting” us or something, and I kind of laughed because it was stupid. But then right after that I was thinking about how the ground was too soft there and the mower was gonna leave tracks if we weren’t careful. That happened at a property last year and the lady called three times about it, even though it dried out fine.

So… I don’t know. I guess I’ve had moments where something wasn’t awful, maybe a quick laugh or something. But pleasure, like really feeling it? I’m not sure I could say.

Even on days that should feel lighter, I don’t feel like I’m really back to myself.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've recently done that you felt gave you pleasure?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… recently? But pleasure… that’s hard to say.\n\nSometimes you get a little relief when a job’s finished, like when the lines are clean and everything’s edged right, but then I’m already thinking about the next thing or what still needs doing. And my brother talks forever about his grill setup—he’s got all these opinions about charcoal versus propane, like it’s a religion—so I sort of just stand there and nod.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed something that felt pleasurable exactly. More like I’m just getting through it.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any recent situation where you felt genuinely happy or satisfied?\n\nPatient: Genuinely happy… I don’t know. I keep trying to think of something, like maybe when I’m with my kids or when the crew is joking around in the truck, you’d think that would do it.\n\nBut most days I wake up and it’s like this gray weight is already sitting on me before anything even happens. And then I go through the day, get out to the sites, talk to people, do what I’m supposed to do… but there’s this low, hollow feeling underneath it. Like I’m there, but I’m not really in it.\n\nI mean, sometimes I smile or laugh if somebody says something stupid at work, but satisfied? Happy? It doesn’t really stay. It’s more like I’m just moving through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall any activity, no matter how small, that you've done in the past few weeks where you felt a sense of enjoyment or satisfaction?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think… something small. I mean, there’s always little things I do. I sharpened the mower blades the other day, but that’s not really enjoyment, that’s just something that has to get done or else the cut looks bad and then people complain.\n\nI guess maybe when something looks neat for a second, like the edge along a walkway, I can look at it and think, “Alright, that’s clean.” But it doesn’t really feel like satisfaction the way I think you mean. It’s more like checking a box.\n\nAnd then my mind goes off to the next yard, or whether the truck needs work—because the truck’s been making this sound that I keep telling myself is probably nothing, but you know how that goes.\n\nSo… no, I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed enjoyment, not in a way that sticks out.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any lack of interest in activities you used to find enjoyable over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. Like… there are things I used to do, sure, but lately I don’t really think about whether I’m interested or not. I just kind of do what needs doing.\n\nI mean, I used to mess around more in the yard at home, not just work stuff, but my own little projects. Then again, when you do landscaping all day, the last thing you want is to come home and look at more edging and mulch. My neighbor’s always out there with his leaf blower like he’s clearing a runway, and I just think, man, how do you still have the energy for that?\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed a specific thing where I thought, “I don’t like this anymore.” It’s more like I don’t stop long enough to check.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days in the past two weeks where you felt interested in the things you were doing?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “interested” is a hard word. I show up and do the things, you know? If I’m laying sod or trimming hedges, I’m paying attention enough so I don’t mess it up or cut something I shouldn’t.\n\nBut interested like I’m into it? I can’t really say. Some days the guys start talking about equipment or whatever, and I’ll listen for a minute, but then I’m thinking about whether we’ve got enough gas cans or if somebody moved the rake again. There’s this one rake that keeps disappearing on the job sites, and I swear it’s like nobody knows how to put anything back.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really had a day where I noticed feeling interested. Mostly I’m just getting through what’s in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt a lack of pleasure in activities that normally would bring you joy over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: I guess… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. Like, “normally would bring me joy” — I’m not sure what even counts anymore. I do things because they’re there to do, or because if I don’t do them, they pile up.\n\nSometimes something should feel good, I suppose. Like finishing a yard and everything’s trimmed up right, or getting home and sitting down for a minute. But I don’t really stop and think, “Oh, this is pleasure.” Mostly I’m thinking about the next thing, or whether I forgot to load something back on the trailer. There was this one time we left a spool of line at a client’s place and had to drive all the way back, and ever since then I’m always checking the trailer twice.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed it in a clear way. I can’t really point to something and say, yeah, that used to bring me joy and now it doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days in the past two weeks where you felt completely back to normal?\n\nPatient: Completely back to normal? No… I don’t think so.\n\nI mean, I get up, I go to work, I’m around the crew, I see my kids, I do the regular stuff. From the outside it probably looks normal enough. But most days it’s like I wake up and there’s already this gray weight sitting on me. It’s hard to explain. Like I’m there, I’m doing things, but there’s this low, hollow feeling underneath it.\n\nEven when something’s happening around me — the guys joking on a job, or the kids talking about whatever they’re into — I’m kind of just going through the motions. Like I’m present but not really… all the way there. So no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had a day where I felt completely back to normal.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt a lack of pleasure in social interactions over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Social interactions… I don’t know. I mean, I talk to people because they’re there, or because we’re working and somebody has to say where the mower’s going or who’s grabbing the bags.\n\nPleasure, though… that’s kind of hard for me to pin down. Sometimes the guys will be joking around, and I’ll hear it, maybe even kind of smile, but then I’m thinking about whether we finished the back fence line or if the client wanted the clippings hauled off. People get real particular about that stuff, and then later they act like they told you when they didn’t.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I can’t say I’ve really noticed whether I’m getting pleasure out of talking to people. It’s mostly just something that happens while I’m getting through the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt a lack of pleasure in doing things that you normally enjoy over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that any different than before. I mean… I do things. I go through the list, I get stuff done. But “normally enjoy” is hard, because I don’t really keep a list of things I enjoy, you know?\n\nLike with work, sometimes a yard looks good when it’s done, the edges are clean, no weeds sticking up around the mailbox, and that should probably feel satisfying. But then I’m already thinking about the next place, or if the blower battery’s charged, or if one of the guys put the rake back in the wrong spot again. We had this whole mess once where a rake fell off because it wasn’t strapped right, and ever since then I’m always checking.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can’t really say I’ve noticed a clear lack of pleasure. I’m just kind of doing the things I do.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt any pleasure at all in the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Any pleasure at all… I mean, maybe little bits, but I don’t really stop and think of it that way.\n\nLike the other day one of the guys made some dumb joke about a sprinkler head looking like it was “saluting” us or something, and I kind of laughed because it was stupid. But then right after that I was thinking about how the ground was too soft there and the mower was gonna leave tracks if we weren’t careful. That happened at a property last year and the lady called three times about it, even though it dried out fine.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I guess I’ve had moments where something wasn’t awful, maybe a quick laugh or something. But pleasure, like really feeling it? I’m not sure I could say.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it changes exactly. It’s been kind of the same thing dragging on.

It started, or at least I noticed it, around when the groundskeeping contract changed our schedule. That was… about three years and a few months ago now, maybe three years and three months. The longer days just kind of took over everything. We used to have a little more room in the day, even just to stop somewhere after work or get home before it felt like the whole night was gone.

Then the schedule shifted, and it was like everything got built around that. Different start times, more properties, everybody rushing, equipment getting loaded wrong because people are tired—stuff like that adds up. So the intensity… I don’t know. It’s been heavy for a long time, since then. Not like it just came out of nowhere last week.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I’m here because most days I wake up and it’s like there’s this gray weight already sitting on me before I even get out of bed. I go to work with the crew, do the landscaping, keep moving, and I’m around my kids too, but it doesn’t really lift. It’s like I’m there, but not really there… just going through the motions.\n\nI don’t know, sometimes I’ll be trimming hedges or whatever and I’ll catch myself thinking about how the grass is greener on one side because of the sprinkler pattern, and then I realize I haven’t felt anything normal all morning. It’s just this low, hollow feeling that sticks around.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying the things you used to enjoy, like spending time with your kids or doing landscaping work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I still do those things. I still show up, take the kids where they need to go, cut lawns, plant stuff, all that. Enjoying it, though… that’s harder to say.\n\nLike with landscaping, sometimes I’ll notice a clean edge on a walkway and think, yeah, that came out right, but then I’m already thinking about how the soil’s too dry or the mower’s acting up again. And with the kids, I want to be there, I do, but I’m not sure I feel it the way I’m supposed to. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel pleasure or interest in activities that you're currently doing?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I still do things, you know? Like I’ll be out with the crew and there’s a yard where the lines come out straight, and for a second I notice it. But then my mind goes off to whether the edging is uneven or if we’re behind schedule.\n\nWith my kids, I want to care about what we’re doing, and I do care about them, obviously, but pleasure… interest… I don’t know. It’s like trying to tell if a plant’s alive in winter. Maybe there’s something there, but it’s hard for me to feel it the way I think I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most of the day, I’d say. It’s not always like crying sad, exactly. It’s more like I wake up and there’s already this gray heaviness sitting on me.\n\nThen I go out with the crew, and I’m mowing or trimming or checking a sprinkler head, and I’m doing the work, but it’s still there. Same when I’m with my kids… I’m there, but it feels kind of hollow, like I’m just moving through it instead of really being in it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but no longer find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can name it exactly. I mean, I used to do landscaping even when I wasn’t working sometimes—mess around in the yard, fix up a bed just because it looked bad—but now I mostly do it because it needs doing.\n\nBut then again, everybody gets tired of work after doing it for years, right? Like my knees hurt after a long day, and the mower we’ve got has been acting up, and one of the guys keeps leaving tools in the truck bed instead of putting them back where they go, so by the time I get home I don’t know if it’s that I don’t enjoy things or I’m just worn out.\n\nWith the kids, too… I don’t know. I’m there. I go. I do the thing. I just can’t tell if “pleasurable” is the word I’d use anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this gray heaviness and emptiness?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over three years, I guess. About three years and a few months.\n\nIt kind of started when the groundskeeping contract changed and our schedule got stretched out. Longer days, different pace, everything sort of took over. At first I thought I was just tired from work, because, you know, when you’re out there all day and the grass is growing faster than you can keep up with… but it never really lifted. So yeah, about three years and three months, if I’m counting right.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of even a few days where this gray heaviness lifted and you felt more like yourself?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I keep thinking maybe there were little breaks, like if we finished a big property early or the weather cooled off for a day, but the heaviness didn’t really go away.\n\nIt’s been there since that contract changed—about three years and three months ago, I guess. Once the longer days took over, it just kind of settled in. Even on days that should feel lighter, I don’t feel like I’m really back to myself.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've recently done that you felt gave you pleasure?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… recently? I mean, I’ve done things. I went over to my brother’s for a cookout, and I fixed this little patch by the side fence at home because the dirt was washing out, and it did look better after. But pleasure… that’s hard to say.\n\nSometimes you get a little relief when a job’s finished, like when the lines are clean and everything’s edged right, but then I’m already thinking about the next thing or what still needs doing. And my brother talks forever about his grill setup—he’s got all these opinions about charcoal versus propane, like it’s a religion—so I sort of just stand there and nod.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed something that felt pleasurable exactly. More like I’m just getting through it.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any recent situation where you felt genuinely happy or satisfied?\n\nPatient: Genuinely happy… I don’t know. I keep trying to think of something, like maybe when I’m with my kids or when the crew is joking around in the truck, you’d think that would do it.\n\nBut most days I wake up and it’s like this gray weight is already sitting on me before anything even happens. And then I go through the day, get out to the sites, talk to people, do what I’m supposed to do… but there’s this low, hollow feeling underneath it. Like I’m there, but I’m not really in it.\n\nI mean, sometimes I smile or laugh if somebody says something stupid at work, but satisfied? Happy? It doesn’t really stay. It’s more like I’m just moving through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall any activity, no matter how small, that you've done in the past few weeks where you felt a sense of enjoyment or satisfaction?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think… something small. I mean, there’s always little things I do. I sharpened the mower blades the other day, but that’s not really enjoyment, that’s just something that has to get done or else the cut looks bad and then people complain.\n\nI guess maybe when something looks neat for a second, like the edge along a walkway, I can look at it and think, “Alright, that’s clean.” But it doesn’t really feel like satisfaction the way I think you mean. It’s more like checking a box.\n\nAnd then my mind goes off to the next yard, or whether the truck needs work—because the truck’s been making this sound that I keep telling myself is probably nothing, but you know how that goes.\n\nSo… no, I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed enjoyment, not in a way that sticks out.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any lack of interest in activities you used to find enjoyable over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. Like… there are things I used to do, sure, but lately I don’t really think about whether I’m interested or not. I just kind of do what needs doing.\n\nI mean, I used to mess around more in the yard at home, not just work stuff, but my own little projects. Then again, when you do landscaping all day, the last thing you want is to come home and look at more edging and mulch. My neighbor’s always out there with his leaf blower like he’s clearing a runway, and I just think, man, how do you still have the energy for that?\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed a specific thing where I thought, “I don’t like this anymore.” It’s more like I don’t stop long enough to check.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days in the past two weeks where you felt interested in the things you were doing?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “interested” is a hard word. I show up and do the things, you know? If I’m laying sod or trimming hedges, I’m paying attention enough so I don’t mess it up or cut something I shouldn’t.\n\nBut interested like I’m into it? I can’t really say. Some days the guys start talking about equipment or whatever, and I’ll listen for a minute, but then I’m thinking about whether we’ve got enough gas cans or if somebody moved the rake again. There’s this one rake that keeps disappearing on the job sites, and I swear it’s like nobody knows how to put anything back.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really had a day where I noticed feeling interested. Mostly I’m just getting through what’s in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt a lack of pleasure in activities that normally would bring you joy over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: I guess… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. Like, “normally would bring me joy” — I’m not sure what even counts anymore. I do things because they’re there to do, or because if I don’t do them, they pile up.\n\nSometimes something should feel good, I suppose. Like finishing a yard and everything’s trimmed up right, or getting home and sitting down for a minute. But I don’t really stop and think, “Oh, this is pleasure.” Mostly I’m thinking about the next thing, or whether I forgot to load something back on the trailer. There was this one time we left a spool of line at a client’s place and had to drive all the way back, and ever since then I’m always checking the trailer twice.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed it in a clear way. I can’t really point to something and say, yeah, that used to bring me joy and now it doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days in the past two weeks where you felt completely back to normal?\n\nPatient: Completely back to normal? No… I don’t think so.\n\nI mean, I get up, I go to work, I’m around the crew, I see my kids, I do the regular stuff. From the outside it probably looks normal enough. But most days it’s like I wake up and there’s already this gray weight sitting on me. It’s hard to explain. Like I’m there, I’m doing things, but there’s this low, hollow feeling underneath it.\n\nEven when something’s happening around me — the guys joking on a job, or the kids talking about whatever they’re into — I’m kind of just going through the motions. Like I’m present but not really… all the way there. So no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had a day where I felt completely back to normal.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt a lack of pleasure in social interactions over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Social interactions… I don’t know. I mean, I talk to people because they’re there, or because we’re working and somebody has to say where the mower’s going or who’s grabbing the bags.\n\nPleasure, though… that’s kind of hard for me to pin down. Sometimes the guys will be joking around, and I’ll hear it, maybe even kind of smile, but then I’m thinking about whether we finished the back fence line or if the client wanted the clippings hauled off. People get real particular about that stuff, and then later they act like they told you when they didn’t.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I can’t say I’ve really noticed whether I’m getting pleasure out of talking to people. It’s mostly just something that happens while I’m getting through the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt a lack of pleasure in doing things that you normally enjoy over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that any different than before. I mean… I do things. I go through the list, I get stuff done. But “normally enjoy” is hard, because I don’t really keep a list of things I enjoy, you know?\n\nLike with work, sometimes a yard looks good when it’s done, the edges are clean, no weeds sticking up around the mailbox, and that should probably feel satisfying. But then I’m already thinking about the next place, or if the blower battery’s charged, or if one of the guys put the rake back in the wrong spot again. We had this whole mess once where a rake fell off because it wasn’t strapped right, and ever since then I’m always checking.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can’t really say I’ve noticed a clear lack of pleasure. I’m just kind of doing the things I do.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt any pleasure at all in the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Any pleasure at all… I mean, maybe little bits, but I don’t really stop and think of it that way.\n\nLike the other day one of the guys made some dumb joke about a sprinkler head looking like it was “saluting” us or something, and I kind of laughed because it was stupid. But then right after that I was thinking about how the ground was too soft there and the mower was gonna leave tracks if we weren’t careful. My neighbor’s always out there with his leaf blower like he’s clearing a runway, and I just think, man, how do you still have the energy for that?\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed a specific thing where I thought, “I don’t like this anymore.” It’s more like I don’t stop long enough to check.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days in the past two weeks where you felt interested in the things you were doing?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “interested” is a hard word. I show up and do the things, you know? If I’m laying sod or trimming hedges, I’m paying attention enough so I don’t mess it up or cut something I shouldn’t.\n\nBut interested like I’m into it? I can’t really say. Some days the guys start talking about equipment or whatever, and I’ll listen for a minute, but then I’m thinking about whether we’ve got enough gas cans or if somebody moved the rake again. There’s this one rake that keeps disappearing on the job sites, and I swear it’s like nobody knows how to put anything back.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really had a day where I noticed feeling interested. Mostly I’m just getting through what’s in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt a lack of pleasure in activities that normally would bring you joy over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: I guess… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. Like, “normally would bring me joy” — I’m not sure what even counts anymore. I do things because they’re there to do, or because if I don’t do them, they pile up.\n\nSometimes something should feel good, I suppose. Like finishing a yard and everything’s trimmed up right, or getting home and sitting down for a minute. But I don’t really stop and think, “Oh, this is pleasure.” Mostly I’m thinking about the next thing, or whether I forgot to load something back on the trailer. There was this one time we left a spool of line at a client’s place and had to drive all the way back, and ever since then I’m always checking the trailer twice.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed it in a clear way. I can’t really point to something and say, yeah, that used to bring me joy and now it doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days in the past two weeks where you felt completely back to normal?\n\nPatient: Completely back to normal? No… I don’t think so.\n\nI mean, I get up, I go to work, I’m around the crew, I see my kids, I do the regular stuff. From the outside it probably looks normal enough. But most days it’s like I wake up and there’s already this gray weight sitting on me. It’s hard to explain. Like I’m there, I’m doing things, but there’s this low, hollow feeling underneath it.\n\nEven when something’s happening around me — the guys joking on a job, or the kids talking about whatever they’re into — I’m kind of just going through the motions. Like I’m present but not really… all the way there. So no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had a day where I felt completely back to normal.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt a lack of pleasure in social interactions over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Social interactions… I don’t know. I mean, I talk to people because they’re there, or because we’re working and somebody has to say where the mower’s going or who’s grabbing the bags.\n\nPleasure, though… that’s kind of hard for me to pin down. Sometimes the guys will be joking around, and I’ll hear it, maybe even kind of smile, but then I’m thinking about whether we finished the back fence line or if the client wanted the clippings hauled off. People get real particular about that stuff, and then later they act like they told you when they didn’t.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I can’t say I’ve really noticed whether I’m getting pleasure out of talking to people. It’s mostly just something that happens while I’m getting through the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt a lack of pleasure in doing things that you normally enjoy over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that any different than before. I mean… I do things. I go through the list, I get stuff done. But “normally enjoy” is hard, because I don’t really keep a list of things I enjoy, you know?\n\nLike with work, sometimes a yard looks good when it’s done, the edges are clean, no weeds sticking up around the mailbox, and that should probably feel satisfying. But then I’m already thinking about the next place, or if the blower battery’s charged, or if one of the guys put the rake back in the wrong spot again. We had this whole mess once where a rake fell off because it wasn’t strapped right, and ever since then I’m always checking.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can’t really say I’ve noticed a clear lack of pleasure. I’m just kind of doing the things I do.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt any pleasure at all in the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Any pleasure at all… I mean, maybe little bits, but I don’t really stop and think of it that way.\n\nLike the other day one of the guys made some dumb joke about a sprinkler head looking like it was “saluting” us or something, and I kind of laughed because it was stupid. But then right after that I was thinking about how the ground was too soft there and the mower was gonna leave tracks if we weren’t careful. That happened at a property last year and the lady called three times about it, even though it dried out fine.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I guess I’ve had moments where something wasn’t awful, maybe a quick laugh or something. But pleasure, like really feeling it? I’m not sure I could say.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in the intensity of your symptoms over the past three years?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it changes exactly. It’s been kind of the same thing dragging on.\n\nIt started, or at least I noticed it, around when the groundskeeping contract changed our schedule. That was… about three years and a few months ago now, maybe three years and three months. The longer days just kind of took over everything. We used to have a little more room in the day, even just to stop somewhere after work or get home before it felt like the whole night was gone.\n\nThen the schedule shifted, and it was like everything got built around that. Different start times, more properties, everybody rushing, equipment getting loaded wrong because people are tired—stuff like that adds up. So the intensity… I don’t know. It’s been heavy for a long time, since then. Not like it just came out of nowhere last week.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah… two weeks or more, definitely. It’s more like most days than not.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I’m here because most days I wake up and it’s like there’s this gray weight already sitting on me before I even get out of bed. Same when I’m with my kids… I’m there, but it feels kind of hollow, like I’m just moving through it instead of really being in it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but no longer find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can name it exactly. I mean, I used to do landscaping even when I wasn’t working sometimes—mess around in the yard, fix up a bed just because it looked bad—but now I mostly do it because it needs doing.\n\nBut then again, everybody gets tired of work after doing it for years, right? Like my knees hurt after a long day, and the mower we’ve got has been acting up, and one of the guys keeps leaving tools in the truck bed instead of putting them back where they go, so by the time I get home I don’t know if it’s that I don’t enjoy things or I’m just worn out.\n\nWith the kids, too… I don’t know. So yeah, about three years and three months, if I’m counting right.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of even a few days where this gray heaviness lifted and you felt more like yourself?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I keep thinking maybe there were little breaks, like if we finished a big property early or the weather cooled off for a day, but the heaviness didn’t really go away.\n\nIt’s been there since that contract changed—about three years and three months ago, I guess. Once the longer days took over, it just kind of settled in. And my brother talks forever about his grill setup—he’s got all these opinions about charcoal versus propane, like it’s a religion—so I sort of just stand there and nod.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed something that felt pleasurable exactly. More like I’m just getting through it.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any recent situation where you felt genuinely happy or satisfied?\n\nPatient: Genuinely happy… I don’t know. I keep trying to think of something, like maybe when I’m with my kids or when the crew is joking around in the truck, you’d think that would do it.\n\nBut most days I wake up and it’s like this gray weight is already sitting on me before anything even happens. And then I go through the day, get out to the sites, talk to people, do what I’m supposed to do… but there’s this low, hollow feeling underneath it. Like I’m there, but I’m not really in it.\n\nI mean, sometimes I smile or laugh if somebody says something stupid at work, but satisfied? Happy? It doesn’t really stay. It’s more like I’m just moving through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall any activity, no matter how small, that you've done in the past few weeks where you felt a sense of enjoyment or satisfaction?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think… something small. I mean, there’s always little things I do. I sharpened the mower blades the other day, but that’s not really enjoyment, that’s just something that has to get done or else the cut looks bad and then people complain.\n\nI guess maybe when something looks neat for a second, like the edge along a walkway, I can look at it and think, “Alright, that’s clean.” But it doesn’t really feel like satisfaction the way I think you mean. It’s more like checking a box.\n\nAnd then my mind goes off to the next yard, or whether the truck needs work—because the truck’s been making this sound that I keep telling myself is probably nothing, but you know how that goes.\n\nSo… no, I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed enjoyment, not in a way that sticks out.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any lack of interest in activities you used to find enjoyable over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. Like… there are things I used to do, sure, but lately I don’t really think about whether I’m interested or not. I just kind of do what needs doing.\n\nI mean, I used to mess around more in the yard at home, not just work stuff, but my own little projects. Then again, when you do landscaping all day, the last thing you want is to come home and look at more edging and mulch. My neighbor’s always out there with his leaf blower like he’s clearing a runway, and I just think, man, how do you still have the energy for that?\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed a specific thing where I thought, “I don’t like this anymore.” It’s more like I don’t stop long enough to check.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days in the past two weeks where you felt interested in the things you were doing?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “interested” is a hard word. I show up and do the things, you know? If I’m laying sod or trimming hedges, I’m paying attention enough so I don’t mess it up or cut something I shouldn’t.\n\nBut interested like I’m into it? I can’t really say. Some days the guys start talking about equipment or whatever, and I’ll listen for a minute, but then I’m thinking about whether we’ve got enough gas cans or if somebody moved the rake again. There’s this one rake that keeps disappearing on the job sites, and I swear it’s like nobody knows how to put anything back.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really had a day where I noticed feeling interested. Mostly I’m just getting through what’s in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt a lack of pleasure in activities that normally would bring you joy over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: I guess… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. Like, “normally would bring me joy” — I’m not sure what even counts anymore. I do things because they’re there to do, or because if I don’t do them, they pile up.\n\nSometimes something should feel good, I suppose. Like finishing a yard and everything’s trimmed up right, or getting home and sitting down for a minute. But I don’t really stop and think, “Oh, this is pleasure.” Mostly I’m thinking about the next thing, or whether I forgot to load something back on the trailer. There was this one time we left a spool of line at a client’s place and had to drive all the way back, and ever since then I’m always checking the trailer twice.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed it in a clear way. I can’t really point to something and say, yeah, that used to bring me joy and now it doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days in the past two weeks where you felt completely back to normal?\n\nPatient: Completely back to normal? No… I don’t think so.\n\nI mean, I get up, I go to work, I’m around the crew, I see my kids, I do the regular stuff. From the outside it probably looks normal enough. But most days it’s like I wake up and there’s already this gray weight sitting on me. It’s hard to explain. Like I’m there, I’m doing things, but there’s this low, hollow feeling underneath it.\n\nEven when something’s happening around me — the guys joking on a job, or the kids talking about whatever they’re into — I’m kind of just going through the motions. Like I’m present but not really… all the way there. So no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had a day where I felt completely back to normal.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt a lack of pleasure in social interactions over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Social interactions… I don’t know. I mean, I talk to people because they’re there, or because we’re working and somebody has to say where the mower’s going or who’s grabbing the bags.\n\nPleasure, though… that’s kind of hard for me to pin down. Sometimes the guys will be joking around, and I’ll hear it, maybe even kind of smile, but then I’m thinking about whether we finished the back fence line or if the client wanted the clippings hauled off. People get real particular about that stuff, and then later they act like they told you when they didn’t.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I can’t say I’ve really noticed whether I’m getting pleasure out of talking to people. It’s mostly just something that happens while I’m getting through the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt a lack of pleasure in doing things that you normally enjoy over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that any different than before. I mean… I do things. I go through the list, I get stuff done. But “normally enjoy” is hard, because I don’t really keep a list of things I enjoy, you know?\n\nLike with work, sometimes a yard looks good when it’s done, the edges are clean, no weeds sticking up around the mailbox, and that should probably feel satisfying. But then I’m already thinking about the next place, or if the blower battery’s charged, or if one of the guys put the rake back in the wrong spot again. We had this whole mess once where a rake fell off because it wasn’t strapped right, and ever since then I’m always checking.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can’t really say I’ve noticed a clear lack of pleasure. I’m just kind of doing the things I do.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt any pleasure at all in the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Any pleasure at all… I mean, maybe little bits, but I don’t really stop and think of it that way.\n\nLike the other day one of the guys made some dumb joke about a sprinkler head looking like it was “saluting” us or something, and I kind of laughed because it was stupid. But then right after that I was thinking about how the ground was too soft there and the mower was gonna leave tracks if we weren’t careful. That happened at a property last year and the lady called three times about it, even though it dried out fine.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I guess I’ve had moments where something wasn’t awful, maybe a quick laugh or something. But pleasure, like really feeling it? I’m not sure I could say.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in the intensity of your symptoms over the past three years?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it changes exactly. It’s been kind of the same thing dragging on.\n\nIt started, or at least I noticed it, around when the groundskeeping contract changed our schedule. That was… about three years and a few months ago now, maybe three years and three months. The longer days just kind of took over everything. We used to have a little more room in the day, even just to stop somewhere after work or get home before it felt like the whole night was gone.\n\nThen the schedule shifted, and it was like everything got built around that. Different start times, more properties, everybody rushing, equipment getting loaded wrong because people are tired—stuff like that adds up. So the intensity… I don’t know. It’s been heavy for a long time, since then. Not like it just came out of nowhere last week.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced two weeks or more where you felt this gray heaviness nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… two weeks or more, definitely. It’s more like most days than not.\n\nI’ll wake up and it’s already there, before anything even happens, like this gray weight sitting on me. And I can still get up, go to work, talk to the crew, deal with the mowers and the trailer and all that. Like I’m moving through the day because that’s what I’m supposed to do, not because I really feel in it.\n\nSo yeah, if you mean feeling that low heaviness nearly every day for at least two weeks, then yes. I’m not sure I could say.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in the intensity of your symptoms over the past three years?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it changes exactly. It’s been kind of the same thing dragging on.\n\nIt started, or at least I noticed it, around when the groundskeeping contract changed our schedule. That was… about three years and a few months ago now, maybe three years and three months. The longer days just kind of took over everything. We used to have a little more room in the day, even just to stop somewhere after work or get home before it felt like the whole night was gone.\n\nThen the schedule shifted, and it was like everything got built around that. Different start times, more properties, everybody rushing, equipment getting loaded wrong because people are tired—stuff like that adds up. So the intensity… I don’t know. It’s been heavy for a long time, since then. Not like it just came out of nowhere last week.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced two weeks or more where you felt this gray heaviness nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… two weeks or more, definitely. It’s more like most days than not.\n\nI’ll wake up and it’s already there, before anything even happens, like this gray weight sitting on me. And I can still get up, go to work, talk to the crew, deal with the mowers and the trailer and all that. I’m around my kids too, and I try to act normal, because you don’t want them thinking you’re just checked out. But inside it’s like… hollow, I guess. Like I’m moving through the day because that’s what I’m supposed to do, not because I really feel in it.\n\nSo yeah, if you mean feeling that low heaviness nearly every day for at least two weeks, then yes. It’s been like that.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you felt this way for most of the day, nearly every day, for the past two years or more?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 48 / landscaping_or_groundskeeping_worker / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Duration\nmanifestation: \"This has been going on for about 3 years and 3 months, since the groundskeeping contract changed our schedule and the longer days took over my life.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I’d say so. It’s been most of the day, most days, for… well, about three years and three months now, give or take.

It kind of tracks back to when the groundskeeping contract changed our schedule. The longer days took over everything, and after a while it was like my whole life got arranged around work and being worn down. I remember at first thinking, “Okay, it’s just a busy stretch,” like when spring hits and everybody wants their lawns perfect all at once… but it never really let up.

So, yeah. More than two years. It’s been going on since that schedule changed.

It’s like I’m there, but not really there… just going through the motions.\n\nI don’t know, sometimes I’ll be trimming hedges or whatever and I’ll catch myself thinking about how the grass is greener on one side because of the sprinkler pattern, and then I realize I haven’t felt anything normal all morning. Like finishing a yard and everything’s trimmed up right, or getting home and sitting down for a minute. I can’t really say I’ve noticed a clear lack of pleasure. I’m just kind of doing the things I do.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt any pleasure at all in the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Any pleasure at all… I mean, maybe little bits, but I don’t really stop and think of it that way.\n\nLike the other day one of the guys made some dumb joke about a sprinkler head looking like it was “saluting” us or something, and I kind of laughed because it was stupid. But then right after that I was thinking about how the ground was too soft there and the mower was gonna leave tracks if we weren’t careful. That happened at a property last year and the lady called three times about it, even though it dried out fine.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I guess I’ve had moments where something wasn’t awful, maybe a quick laugh or something. But pleasure, like really feeling it? I keep trying to think of something, like maybe when I’m with my kids or when the crew is joking around in the truck, you’d think that would do it.\n\nBut most days I wake up and it’s like this gray weight is already sitting on me before anything even happens. And then I go through the day, get out to the sites, talk to people, do what I’m supposed to do… but there’s this low, hollow feeling underneath it. Like I’m there, but I’m not really in it.\n\nI mean, sometimes I smile or laugh if somebody says something stupid at work, but satisfied? Happy? It doesn’t really stay. It’s more like I’m just moving through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall any activity, no matter how small, that you've done in the past few weeks where you felt a sense of enjoyment or satisfaction?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think… something small. I mean, there’s always little things I do. I sharpened the mower blades the other day, but that’s not really enjoyment, that’s just something that has to get done or else the cut looks bad and then people complain.\n\nI guess maybe when something looks neat for a second, like the edge along a walkway, I can look at it and think, “Alright, that’s clean.” But it doesn’t really feel like satisfaction the way I think you mean. It’s more like checking a box.\n\nAnd then my mind goes off to the next yard, or whether the truck needs work—because the truck’s been making this sound that I keep telling myself is probably nothing, but you know how that goes.\n\nSo… no, I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed enjoyment, not in a way that sticks out.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any lack of interest in activities you used to find enjoyable over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. Like… there are things I used to do, sure, but lately I don’t really think about whether I’m interested or not. I just kind of do what needs doing.\n\nI mean, I used to mess around more in the yard at home, not just work stuff, but my own little projects. Then again, when you do landscaping all day, the last thing you want is to come home and look at more edging and mulch. My neighbor’s always out there with his leaf blower like he’s clearing a runway, and I just think, man, how do you still have the energy for that?\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed a specific thing where I thought, “I don’t like this anymore.” It’s more like I don’t stop long enough to check.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days in the past two weeks where you felt interested in the things you were doing?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “interested” is a hard word. I show up and do the things, you know? If I’m laying sod or trimming hedges, I’m paying attention enough so I don’t mess it up or cut something I shouldn’t.\n\nBut interested like I’m into it? I can’t really say. Some days the guys start talking about equipment or whatever, and I’ll listen for a minute, but then I’m thinking about whether we’ve got enough gas cans or if somebody moved the rake again. There’s this one rake that keeps disappearing on the job sites, and I swear it’s like nobody knows how to put anything back.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really had a day where I noticed feeling interested. Mostly I’m just getting through what’s in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt a lack of pleasure in activities that normally would bring you joy over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: I guess… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. Like, “normally would bring me joy” — I’m not sure what even counts anymore. I do things because they’re there to do, or because if I don’t do them, they pile up.\n\nSometimes something should feel good, I suppose. Like finishing a yard and everything’s trimmed up right, or getting home and sitting down for a minute. But I don’t really stop and think, “Oh, this is pleasure.” Mostly I’m thinking about the next thing, or whether I forgot to load something back on the trailer. There was this one time we left a spool of line at a client’s place and had to drive all the way back, and ever since then I’m always checking the trailer twice.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed it in a clear way. I can’t really point to something and say, yeah, that used to bring me joy and now it doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days in the past two weeks where you felt completely back to normal?\n\nPatient: Completely back to normal? No… I don’t think so.\n\nI mean, I get up, I go to work, I’m around the crew, I see my kids, I do the regular stuff. From the outside it probably looks normal enough. But most days it’s like I wake up and there’s already this gray weight sitting on me. It’s hard to explain. Like I’m there, I’m doing things, but there’s this low, hollow feeling underneath it.\n\nEven when something’s happening around me — the guys joking on a job, or the kids talking about whatever they’re into — I’m kind of just going through the motions. Like I’m present but not really… all the way there. So no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had a day where I felt completely back to normal.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt a lack of pleasure in social interactions over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Social interactions… I don’t know. I mean, I talk to people because they’re there, or because we’re working and somebody has to say where the mower’s going or who’s grabbing the bags.\n\nPleasure, though… that’s kind of hard for me to pin down. Sometimes the guys will be joking around, and I’ll hear it, maybe even kind of smile, but then I’m thinking about whether we finished the back fence line or if the client wanted the clippings hauled off. People get real particular about that stuff, and then later they act like they told you when they didn’t.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I can’t say I’ve really noticed whether I’m getting pleasure out of talking to people. It’s mostly just something that happens while I’m getting through the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt a lack of pleasure in doing things that you normally enjoy over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that any different than before. I mean… I do things. I go through the list, I get stuff done. But “normally enjoy” is hard, because I don’t really keep a list of things I enjoy, you know?\n\nLike with work, sometimes a yard looks good when it’s done, the edges are clean, no weeds sticking up around the mailbox, and that should probably feel satisfying. But then I’m already thinking about the next place, or if the blower battery’s charged, or if one of the guys put the rake back in the wrong spot again. We had this whole mess once where a rake fell off because it wasn’t strapped right, and ever since then I’m always checking.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can’t really say I’ve noticed a clear lack of pleasure. I’m just kind of doing the things I do.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt any pleasure at all in the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Any pleasure at all… I mean, maybe little bits, but I don’t really stop and think of it that way.\n\nLike the other day one of the guys made some dumb joke about a sprinkler head looking like it was “saluting” us or something, and I kind of laughed because it was stupid. But then right after that I was thinking about how the ground was too soft there and the mower was gonna leave tracks if we weren’t careful. That happened at a property last year and the lady called three times about it, even though it dried out fine.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I guess I’ve had moments where something wasn’t awful, maybe a quick laugh or something. But pleasure, like really feeling it? I’m not sure I could say.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in the intensity of your symptoms over the past three years?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it changes exactly. It’s been kind of the same thing dragging on.\n\nIt started, or at least I noticed it, around when the groundskeeping contract changed our schedule. That was… about three years and a few months ago now, maybe three years and three months. The longer days just kind of took over everything. We used to have a little more room in the day, even just to stop somewhere after work or get home before it felt like the whole night was gone.\n\nThen the schedule shifted, and it was like everything got built around that. Different start times, more properties, everybody rushing, equipment getting loaded wrong because people are tired—stuff like that adds up. So the intensity… I don’t know. It’s been heavy for a long time, since then. Not like it just came out of nowhere last week.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced two weeks or more where you felt this gray heaviness nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… two weeks or more, definitely. It’s more like most days than not.\n\nI’ll wake up and it’s already there, before anything even happens, like this gray weight sitting on me. And I can still get up, go to work, talk to the crew, deal with the mowers and the trailer and all that. I’m around my kids too, and I try to act normal, because you don’t want them thinking you’re just checked out. But inside it’s like… hollow, I guess. Like I’m moving through the day because that’s what I’m supposed to do, not because I really feel in it.\n\nSo yeah, if you mean feeling that low heaviness nearly every day for at least two weeks, then yes. It’s been like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this way for most of the day, nearly every day, for the past two years or more?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say so. It’s been most of the day, most days, for… well, about three years and three months now, give or take.\n\nIt kind of tracks back to when the groundskeeping contract changed our schedule. The longer days took over everything, and after a while it was like my whole life got arranged around work and being worn down. I remember at first thinking, “Okay, it’s just a busy stretch,” like when spring hits and everybody wants their lawns perfect all at once… but it never really let up.\n\nSo, yeah. More than two years. It’s been going on since that schedule changed."
